- **Tool** (bridge `present`, `docs/bridge-protocol.md` "Presented artifacts"). `{title, path | content, kind?, language?}`; the bridge snapshots the document into the session's `local://` root as `presented/<slug>-v<n>.<ext>` (0600, `O_EXCL`; presenting the same title again is the next version) and copies the files it references by relative path (stylesheets, scripts, images; inside its folder only, 64 files, 4 MiB each, 16 MiB together) to `presented/<slug>-v<n>.files/`. The snapshots live and move with the session (`omp session pack` takes `local://`), so the transcript shows each version as it was, whatever happens to the source. Nothing crosses the bridge socket for it.
- **Typing** (`tf-collab` `replica/tools.rs` `presented`). A finished, successful `present` row carries `ToolItem.artifact: PresentedArtifact {title, kind, name, url, slug, version, size, language?, source?, assets}` from the result's `details`, only when `name` is a snapshot name whose slug and version agree with the details. Live (collab and bridge data planes) and rebuilt rows (parked sessions, subagent transcripts, `transcript_page`) get it from the same code. Apps before 0.9.0 ignore the field and show a generic tool row.
- **Reading** (`session.artifact`, §3.20). `base64: true` answers any file's bytes as `data` (8 MiB at most; hosts before 0.9.0 ignore the flag and answer text or `binary`, so images say "Update this host"). `.html` files list their `<title>`.
- **App.** A `present` row is a card in the transcript (kind icon, title, version, size, Open). Open shows the document in the split panel beside the session (`SplitState` kind `artifact`; the panel mechanism every side panel uses: `SplitFrame` with `SplitHeaderRow`, the browser's panel too), with Full screen (`FullscreenFrame`); on phones and in focus mode it opens full screen. The inspector's Artifacts list (rail and phone tab) groups presented documents under "Presented" (one row per title, its newest version; the `.files/` copies are not listed) and opens every file in the same viewer. The viewer has a version switcher (every `presented/<slug>-v<n>.*` of the listing; what the agent said a version is carries over to another only when both have the same extension, so v1 a page and v2 Markdown shows v2 as Markdown), Copy, Save (not on Android), Open in browser (desktop: `doc_open` writes a temporary file under `<temp>/teleflock-docs-<uid>/<random>/` on Linux and macOS, `%TEMP%\teleflock-docs\<random>\` on Windows (0700/0600; one folder per account, as `/tmp` is shared on Linux; a folder that is a symlink or another user's is refused; folders older than 24 h are deleted) and opens it with the system's handler), Copy path and Mention. Save and Open in browser name the file by what the viewer shows (`docFileName`), never with an extension a default app would run or render outside the sandbox: a page `.html`, written as its copy with the policy first (below); a drawing `.svg`, and for Open in browser `.html`, a page under the policy that shows the drawing as an `<img>`; Markdown `.md`, JSON `.json`, text `.txt`, an image its own type; code its source extension when a default app shows it as text, else `.txt` appended (`report.html` presented as code is `report.html.txt`); a table `.csv`/`.tsv`, for Open in browser with `.txt` appended (a spreadsheet app would evaluate its formulas). `doc_open` keeps only those extensions and appends `.txt` to anything else (`.svg`, `.xml`, `.csv`, `.bat`, `.js`, `.py`, `.command`, `.desktop` …), so the handler never runs a program or a script the agent presented. Renderers: HTML in the sandbox below; Markdown with the transcript's renderer (diagrams and math, §6.11; images it references inlined); SVG and images as `<img>` (zoom in the lightbox); code highlighted; CSV/TSV as a table (2,000 rows, 100 columns; a note says how many columns there are); JSON pretty-printed; text.
- **Sandbox** (`artifacts/sandbox.ts`). A page runs in two nested frames, both `sandbox="allow-scripts"` and never `allow-same-origin`, so its scripts run in an opaque origin: no access to the app's DOM, storage, cookies or IPC, no popups, forms, downloads, modals or top navigation. The outer frame is a wrapper document whose Content-Security-Policy allows no network loads (`default-src 'none'`; scripts, styles, images, fonts and media only inline or from `data:`/`blob:`; `connect-src`, `frame-src`, `form-action`, `base-uri` `'none'`); the page is the wrapper's `srcdoc` frame, inherits that policy and repeats it ahead of all of its own markup (right after its doctype when that comes first, else at the very start; comments ended where the HTML tokenizer ends them, so neither a `<head>` inside a comment or a script nor a script before the page's head comes first), with a prelude that runs before any of the page's scripts: it removes WebRTC and drops the resource hints the page's scripts add through the DOM (below), and turns link clicks into a message to the app. In a browser the wrapper is itself an `srcdoc` frame; in the desktop app it is served from the `tfdoc:` scheme (src-tauri `docs.rs`: `tfdoc://localhost/<token>`, on Windows `http://tfdoc.localhost/<token>`; in memory, at most 12 documents and 96 MiB, 128-bit token, response headers with the same policy plus `sandbox allow-scripts`), because an `srcdoc` frame there would inherit the app's own CSP, which blocks inline scripts. On Windows wry also puts Tauri's scripts into frames; `connect-src 'none'` in both frames and Tauri refusing IPC from a `null` origin keep the page away from the app's commands. **Android** shows pages without scripts (both frames `sandbox=""`, plain `srcdoc`, a note says so): its WebView injects Tauri's IPC scripts into every frame (wry adds them for all frames on Android, and Tauri attributes IPC to the top-level page), so a page whose scripts run could reach the app's commands there (read from the wry and Tauri sources, not run on a device). Files the page references are read with `session.artifact {base64}` from its `.files/` folder and inlined as `data:` URLs (the bridge's limits; stylesheets' own `url()`/`@import` two levels deep); anything else it references (CDNs, remote images, fetch) does not load. Every scan of the page's markup (references, hints, the place for the policy), of its stylesheets and of Markdown is linear in its length; a tag the markup never ends ends the scan.
- **What CSP doesn't cover.** WebRTC (STUN over UDP, TURN over TCP to a host the page names) and resource hints: under `default-src 'none'` Chromium 150 and WebKitGTK 2.54 open a `<link rel=preconnect>`'s connection (a DNS lookup, the TCP and TLS handshakes; no request) and Chromium sends a `<link rel=prerender>`'s request in full (observed with `scripts/lab/artifacts` and stand-alone probes; WebView2 and WKWebView, the same engines, not run). So: the page's markup loses the `rel` tokens `preconnect`, `dns-prefetch`, `prefetch`, `prerender` and `modulepreload` of its `<link>`s before it is shown or saved (and any token a character reference the viewer doesn't decode leaves with a `&`); the prelude drops the same tokens when the page's scripts set them through the DOM (`rel`, `relList`, `setAttribute(NS)`, attribute nodes and `setNamedItem`) and removes `RTCPeerConnection` and its kin from the page's window; the desktop apps run a script in every frame of an opaque origin before its own scripts (src-tauri `frames.rs`, all frames: WebView2's document-created scripts, WebKit user scripts), which removes WebRTC from the frames a page makes itself too (an `srcdoc` frame runs its own scripts in a fresh window; an empty frame of a sandboxed page is cross-origin to it; observed in Chromium with a script on every new document and in WebKitGTK with an all-frames user script); on Linux WebKitGTK's WebRTC (off by default) and its `LinkPreconnect` feature are switched off. **What a page written to get out still reaches:** resource hints in markup its scripts parse from a string (`innerHTML`, `insertAdjacentHTML`, `document.write`, a `DOMParser` document) or put into a frame they make (`srcdoc`) stay, so on Windows, on macOS and in a browser a preconnect reaches a host the page names (a DNS lookup and a TCP/TLS handshake, its server name included), and in Chromium browsers (and on Windows, unless WebView2 lacks Chromium's prerender: not checked) a prerender sends a GET to it; in a browser (the tf-devserver page) a frame the page makes runs its own scripts with WebRTC. Linux closes both (no preconnect: the engine's switch, observed; no WebRTC; WebKitGTK prefetches no DNS by default, its setting read, not observed on the wire). Nothing the page loads, fetches, sends over a socket or submits gets out, and it never reads or changes TeleFlock's data.
- **Links, Save and Open in browser.** A page's link message asks "Open this link in your browser?" only while the user is using the page: their activation (`navigator.userActivation`) and the focus in the page's frame (a click in the page puts it there; typing in the composer activates the app's window too but leaves the focus outside), and opens only http(s) URLs (`openLink`). When the prompt closes the focus does not go back to the frame and link messages are ignored for 1.5 s, so a page can't keep asking. Save and Open in browser write the page with its files inlined, its resource hints dropped, and the encoding, the policy and the prelude's WebRTC and hint guard first, without the link prelude (its links work as links there); in the system browser the page is not in a sandboxed frame, so a frame it makes there can use WebRTC.
- **Compatibility.** Sessions on bridges before 2.3.0 have no `present` tool (the agent cannot present until it restarts with the new bridge). A host before 0.9.0 serves the rows untyped (generic tool rows) and the files as text only.
- **Tests.** `bun test host/teleflock-bridge/present.test.ts` (slugs, kinds, versions, references a page loads, copies inside the folder and within the limits, symlinks out, argument errors, the session's `local://` root, the registration omp sees); `cargo test -p tf-collab present` (typing, live and from the session file, failed calls); `cargo test -p tf-hostd artifact_files` (base64 reads, page titles); `bun test src/features/session/artifacts` (the policy and sandbox attributes, the Android wrapper without scripts, the escaping of the page into the wrapper, the policy and prelude ahead of all of the page's markup (heads in comments and scripts, comments ended as browsers end them), resource hints dropped from the markup and refused by the prelude on a stand-in DOM, WebRTC removed, the link prompt's conditions, the page Open in browser writes for a drawing, link messages, references and inlining with stylesheets, tags where a browser finds them, linear time on markup that never ends, renderer selection, file names for Save and Open in browser, versions, CSV and its column cap); `bun test src/features/session/markdown` (`prepareSvg` in linear time, too large refused first); `cargo test -p teleflock --lib docs` (src-tauri: URLs per platform, tokens, eviction, names (`.svg`, `.xml`, `.csv` get `.txt`), headers, `doc_open`'s limits, modes, refusals and per-account folder); `cargo test -p teleflock --lib frames` (WebKitGTK's `LinkPreconnect` switch; under a display, e.g. `xvfb-run`, that it turns off); `bun test src/core/demo/present.test.ts` (the demo host's presented documents: rows and listing agree, every referenced file listed, base64 reads, versions 1 and 2 of both titles). Lab: `scripts/lab/artifacts/setup.sh --fresh art-lab` then `bun scripts/lab/artifacts/drive.ts art-lab` (real omp, herdr, hostd and a mock provider: an HTML report twice, Markdown, and a page that tries every way out of the sandbox, WebRTC from the page, an empty frame and a frame it makes and resource hints in its markup and added at runtime included, against a sink that logs what reaches it over HTTP, raw TCP and UDP; in a browser the expected lines are a TLS ClientHello for `html-string.localhost` and UDP/TCP from the frame the page made).

### 3.33 TeleFlock Browser, the host side (`browser.rs`; `agent.browserAttach`, `agent.browserDetach`, `agent.browserAnswer`; wire types `tf_proto::browser`; app core §5.2, UI §6.13)

An app with the TeleFlock Browser (wire feature `browser`: the desktop apps and tf-devserver from 0.9.0) lends it to an omp agent of this herdr session. omp's own `browser` tool drives it: the bridge points the agent's omp at a loopback port hostd listens on (`browser.cdpUrl`, a runtime override), and every connection to that port is a stream to the lending app, whose DevTools proxy shows the agent only its own tabs (§5.2). The agent never reaches the browser's own DevTools port, and the app never reaches the host's network: hostd listens, the app connects to its browser locally.
- **Attach** (`agent.browserAttach {agentId, port?, scope?}` → `{port, cdpUrl}`). Refused for an app without the feature (`unsupported`, "Update TeleFlock on this device"), an agent not on the board (`not_found`) or parked, an omp without a bridge (`not_connected`, "Start the agent from TeleFlock") and a bridge without `caps.browser` (`unsupported`: older than 2.3.0, "Restart the agent to update its bridge"). hostd listens on `127.0.0.1:<port>` (the port asked for; absent, 0 or taken: a free one), sends the bridge `browserOverride {cdpUrl: "http://127.0.0.1:<port>", scope}` (`scope` default `session`; `turn` is "Allow once") and only then records the attachment: one per agent, with the app, port, scope, `since` and the omp session whose bridge holds the override. When the override fails, the listener goes and the error is the answer. The same app attaching again keeps its listener and port (the scope follows the new call); another app's attach replaces it with a new listener, and the previous app hears `browserDetached {agentId, reason: "TeleFlock on <device> took it over"}` and loses its streams. The card carries `Agent.browser.attached {device, since, scope}`.
- **Streams.** Each accepted connection is a forwarding stream (§3.30's ids, flow control and per-name cap of `FWD_STREAMS` = 64 per agent) of kind `browser` towards the lending app: `fwd {sid, ev: open {kind: "browser", name: <agentId>, port}}`; bytes flow once the app answers `opened`, so the agent's HTTP (`/json/version`, `/json/list`, …) and its DevTools WebSocket pass through byte for byte. On Linux only this user's processes may connect (the connecting socket's owner in `/proc/net/tcp`, as for forwarded ports); others are closed at once, never reach the app, and are logged at most once a minute. Browser streams run with the `browser` feature alone (no `forward` needed), always from a host to the app: a host refuses an app's `open` of kind `browser`.
- **Ends.** `agent.browserDetach {agentId}` from any app (the lending app hears "detached in TeleFlock on <device>" unless it asked itself); the lending app disconnecting; the bridge ending a turn's override at the turn's end (`browser_released`: "the agent's turn ended (allowed once)"); the agent's omp session ending (bridge `Disconnected`: "the agent's omp session ended"). Each sends the bridge `browserOverride {cdpUrl: null}` where the bridge is still there, stops the listener, closes the agent's connections (the app's streams get `close` with the reason) and clears the card; the activity log says "Browser detached" for a detach. A session switch in the same omp process (`/new`, `/resume`) keeps it: the override lives in the process.
- **Requests** (`use_user_browser`, docs/bridge-protocol.md "TeleFlock Browser"). The bridge's `browser_request {id, reason, at}` sits on the agent's bridge session until `browser_request_end`, so it reaches the card as `Agent.browser.request {id, reason, at}` and as a needs-you question: `confirm` with `browser: true`, title "Use your browser?", the reason as the question, options `session` "Allow for this session" (primary), `turn` "Allow once", `deny` "Deny" (danger), nothing recommended (decide for me never answers it), and the notification "<agent> wants to use your browser". With no connected app of the session that has the `browser` feature, hostd answers at once with `browserAnswer {allow: false, unavailable: true, message: "No TeleFlock app with a browser is connected to this host right now."}`. **Allow** takes an app with the browser: it attaches with the chosen scope, and the attach answers the request (`browserAnswer {allow: true, scope, cdpUrl}`, activity "Browser allowed for this session" / "… for this turn"); `agent.browserAnswer {requestId, allow: true}` answers it with an attachment that app already made (else `invalid_params`). **Deny** works from any app: `agent.browserAnswer {allow: false}`, or `agent.answer` with choice `deny` or a dismissal (the board, the inbox, Answer next, notification actions, phones); `agent.answer` with an allow choice is `unsupported` ("Allow it in TeleFlock on the computer whose browser the agent should use"). Answers to a request that is over are `not_found`.
- **Compatibility.** Hosts before 0.9.0 answer `agent.browser*` with `unknown_method` and never show a request; apps without the feature get no browser streams and can only deny; an agent whose omp loaded a bridge before 2.3.0 has no `use_user_browser` tool and is refused at attach until it restarts.
- **Tests.** `cargo test -p tf-hostd browser::tests` against a scripted bridge (`bridge::fake`): the listener's lifecycle (refusals, a taken port, a stream to the lending app with bytes both ways, the same app keeping its port, another app taking over, a detach from another app, the lending app detaching its own attachment without being told of it, the card); an attach whose override the bridge refuses (no listener, no attachment) and a bridge without `caps.browser` refused; only this user's processes (as root a forked process of another uid is closed without reaching the app); the ends (the app gone, a turn's release, a session switch kept, the omp session ended); requests (unavailable without a browser app, a phone not counting as one, the card's question and options, an attach allowing once, `agent.browserAnswer` allow and deny, `agent.answer` deny-only, an answer after the end); who answers (any app can deny: one that never lent the browser, one other than the lender, the lender, a dismissal on the board; an allow from anyone but the lending app is refused). The bridge half: `bun test host/teleflock-bridge/browser.test.ts` (the override, Allow once ending after omp settled the turn with the model's note for its next turn, the tool, a fake hostd). End to end, `scripts/lab/browser` (§5.2) with herdr, this host's tf-hostd, tf-devserver, Chrome for Testing and real omp 18.15.0 agents whose own `browser` drives the user's browser through the listener: attach and detach, the card (published a moment after `agent.browserAttach` answers), the request as card, notification and status, Allow for this session, Deny, Allow once ending with the turn (omp's `browser.cdpUrl` override set during it and gone after it, the port closed), two agents at once, take over and hand back.

### 3.34 Agent chat (`agent_chat.rs`; `session.agentChat`, `SessionMeta.agentChat`; bridge 2.3.0 `caps.agentChat`; app `src/features/session/irc`)

The message board shows every message exchanged inside a session's agent tree: omp IRC (`write agent://<id>`, `agent://all`, omp's wake relays and `?progress` notes) between the main agent and its subagents at any depth, and what the user sent a subagent. Before 0.9.0 the session's IRC card read the main transcript only, which holds the main agent's own sends and what reached it; omp's IRC bus is one per process and nothing watched it. Wire types `tf_proto::session::{AgentChatInfo, AgentChatLog, AgentChatMessage}`, `methods::AgentChatParams`, `SubagentCmdParams.broadcast`.
- **Sources.** (1) The bridge's tap (`caps.agentChat`, docs/bridge-protocol.md "Agent chat"): each delivery leg as an `irc` event when it starts and again once omp settled it, with omp's receipt. (2) The session's transcript files, read incrementally on the blocking pool (from the offset read so far, 1 MiB at a time; an unfinished last line waits, a line over 16 MiB is skipped to its newline; a file that shrank is read again, sightings already in the log merge by their keys): the main session file and every subagent transcript under its artifacts dir (`<Id>.jsonl`, regular files; nested ones in the folder of the subagent that started them, beside its transcript, `<Id>/<Id>.<Child>.jsonl`, 6 levels; advisors' `__…` skipped). No other folder is read, not the session's `local://` root (`local/`) nor omp's other artifact folders, and one read looks at 20 000 folder entries at most. Each goes through `tf_collab` `IrcFileScan`: a sender's `write agent://…` call paired with its result (receipts; a send omp refused before delivery is `failed` with omp's text), a recipient's `irc:incoming`, a parent's steer, a `wait` that consumed a message, and in a subagent's transcript a prompt attributed to the user (`attribution: "user"`, stamped by omp with when it was sent). (3) What the app sent a subagent (`agent.subagent` chat), sender `user`, stamped when the request went out, receipt `injected` or `failed` with the error.
- **One message per send.** A sighting merges by its own key (a tap leg's id, a file's entry or tool call: the same sighting read again), by omp's message id, else into the closest message from the same sender with the same text, broadcast flag and recipient within 3 s (30 s for broadcast legs and the user's messages; a recipient's row stamped before omp 18.14 may come up to 10 min late) whose leg for that recipient was not seen from that side yet. Each side (tap, sender's file, recipient's file, the user's request) counts once per recipient, so the same text sent twice stays two messages. omp sends a broadcast as one leg per peer (every running or idle agent of the process but the sender and advisors); the legs are one message listing them. A subagent's prompt cannot tell a message the user sent it alone from one the board sent every running subagent at once: it joins either (the request tells), and rebuilt from the transcripts alone the same text from the user to several subagents within 30 s is one broadcast.
- **Receipts and replies.** Per recipient: `pending` while a tapped leg is in flight, omp's outcome (`injected`, `woken`, `revived`, `failed` with omp's error), else `received` when only the recipient's transcript has it. A wake relay names the message that woke its sender by omp id; `replyTo` is that message's id in the log once its omp id is known (from the recipient's row; learning it changes the relay: its `rev` moves and the change is announced), else the latest message from the relay's recipient to its sender before it. `at` is when the message was sent, as near as the sightings agree (the tap's start, else the narrowest window of the files).
- **The log** (`Log`, one per omp session id). At most 2 000 messages and 16 MiB of text (a body over 256 KiB is cut; the oldest messages go first and the page says `truncated`); 64 logs in memory, least recently used dropped (rebuilt from the files when asked again). `rev` counts changes and each message carries the `rev` of its last change; `epoch` names one log instance (a new one after a tf-hostd restart or eviction). `session.agentChat {agentId, epoch?, after?}` → `AgentChatLog {epoch, rev, messages, reset, truncated, live}`: oldest first, the messages changed after `after` when `epoch` is the log's, else the whole log (`reset`). A fetch reads the files again when the last read is 750 ms old; a parked agent's log comes from its files. `session.open` starts the first read in the background.
- **Meta.** `SessionMeta.agentChat {epoch, rev, count, live}` in every snapshot and `meta` event (coalesced like every meta, at most one per 500 ms), parked agents' too. `live`: the session's bridge taps the bus, so `rev` moves by itself; otherwise the log changes only when fetched and the app fetches again while the board shows.
- **What the files cannot tell** (after a tf-hostd restart, an older bridge, a collab-served session): no `pending`; a wake relay has no sender record (omp sends it, not a `write` call), so it reads `received`, and when the recipient's row lacks omp's `wakeRelay` flag (omp 18.15's `wait` result sometimes does) it shows as a plain reply, still linked by `replyTo`; a user's message reads `received` (the subagent's prompt) and a broadcast of the user is rebuilt as above. Legs sent while no hostd was connected come back from the files the same way.
- **App.** The inspector card and the message board (side panel, full screen on phones), their store, unread and composer: §6.17. The composer sends `agent.subagent` chat to one subagent, or one request per running subagent with `broadcast: true`.
- **Compatibility.** Hosts before 0.9.0 send no `meta.agentChat` and answer `session.agentChat` with `unknown_method`: the board shows the main transcript's IRC rows and says so. Sessions on bridges before 2.3.0 (and collab-served ones) have `live: false` and a log from the files alone. Older apps ignore the field; older hosts ignore `broadcast`.
- **Tests.** `cargo test -p tf-hostd --lib agent_chat` (one message from every side, repeated texts, broadcast legs, a rebuild from files with reply links, the user's messages back from the subagents' prompts live and rebuilt, incremental reads of a growing nested tree with a half-written line, a rewritten file and advisors, epochs, the bounds), `cargo test -p tf-collab irc` (`IrcFileScan` on omp 18.14/18.15 shapes, the user's prompts in subagent transcripts only), `bun test host/teleflock-bridge/teleflock-bridge.test.ts -t "agent chat"` (the tap's legs, flags, failures, ids, one wrapper per process, the `irc` events and `caps.agentChat` against a fake hostd). End to end with real omp 18.15.0: `scripts/lab/agentchat/` (setup.sh, then drive.ts: a mock provider plays Main → Orchestrator → WorkerA, WorkerB with a broadcast, a progress note, a question that wakes a finished worker and omp's wake relay back, a send to an unknown agent, a send to itself, the user's messages from the board; every message once, in order, with sender, recipients, flags, reply link and receipts, live, after `tf-hostd stop` and for an agent on the last release's bridge).

## 4. Companion extension `teleflock-bridge.ts` (owner: BridgeExt)

**Connection.** At load it connects to `bridge.sock` and retries every 5 s while hostd is absent. It never throws into omp: every timer goes through `ctx.setInterval`, and every handler is wrapped in try/catch.

**One bridge per process** (bridge ≥ 1.4.0). The global install and the `-e` copy (possibly different versions) meet in a process-wide slot `globalThis[Symbol.for("teleflock.bridge")]` `{owners: {[endpoint]: {version, stop()}}, cmdCtx?, attachWaiters}`. Per hostd endpoint the highest version owns it (equal versions: the first claimant, i.e. the global copy); only the owner connects, ticks and shows `⇄ teleflock`, and a newer copy that loads later takes over through the old owner's `stop()`. The command context is captured through `/teleflock-control`, a name only 1.4.0+ copies register (older copies keep `/teleflock-attach`, and omp 18.13.1 dispatched that shared name to the legacy global copy in some processes); whichever 1.4.0+ copy omp runs it in stores the context in the slot and the owner reads it from there. While omp compacts, the queued command makes tree/branch/new-session fail fast with a retryable `busy`. hostd covers copies older than 1.4.0, which never negotiate: per pid only the connection with the highest `bridgeVersion` is routed (`bridge.rs` `on_hello`; equal versions: latest `hello`; another pid always takes a session over), and when the board still maps an agent to its previous session right after a switch, `bridge_sid` falls back to the bridge session of the agent's omp pid. Details: `docs/bridge-protocol.md` "One bridge per omp process".

**Protocol** (JSON lines, documented in `docs/bridge-protocol.md`):
- On connect, the extension sends `hello {pid, sessionId, sessionFile, cwd, ompVersion, bridgeVersion, caps?}`. Bridge ≥ 2.0 adds `caps {live, dialogs, subagentCtl, prompt}`; with `caps.live ≥ 1` hostd serves the session from the bridge (§3.4 link lifecycle) and never joins its collab room. Bridge 2.3.0 adds `ompDialogs` (omp's own dialogs, wrapped on its `ExtensionUiController.prototype`: the `cfg://` approvals, the report_tool_issue consent, hook confirmations while omp waits for the user or a TeleFlock command runs, race the TUI like the UI context's) and `userWaits` (`AgentSession.waitForUser` counted; an uncovered wait is `state.userWait`, §3.5).
- **Events** (extension → hostd):
  - `session_switch`
  - `plan_review_requested` / `plan_review_resolved`
  - `model` / `thinking` changes
  - `queue_update`
  - `btw_delta` (per conversation turn; also for `/btw` typed in omp's TUI)
- **Requests** (hostd → extension), answered with `res`:
  - `setModel`, `setThinking`, `setFast`, `setPlan`
  - `getRoleModels`, `setRoleModels` (2.1.0, `caps.roleModels`: the session's own model roles and fallback chains as omp runtime overrides, §3.7)
  - `goal`, `compact`, `rename`
  - `followUp`, `navigateTree`, `branch`, `fork`, `newSession`
  - `resolvePlanReview`, `export`, `handoff`
  - `btw` (omp's own `/btw`, shown in the TUI and its BTW history; `btwId` follows up), `btwCancel`, `getBtwHistory`
  - `getTree`, `getQueue`
- **Data plane** (bridge ≥ 2.0; frames and flow control in `docs/bridge-protocol.md`): `liveSubscribe` answers with a `resync` (everything in flight, `lastEntryId` of the session file) before its `res`, then ordered `live` frames shaped exactly like omp collab host → guest frames (`event`, `bus`, `agents`, `state`, `ui-request`, `ui-request-end`), `seq` +1 per frame per connection; a session switch (`hello {reason: "switch"}`) ends the feed and hostd subscribes again. Requests: `prompt {text, images, delivery}`, `uiResponse {reqId, value}` (collab's exact values; `null` cancels), `agentCmd {cmd, agentId, text?}`. Roster entries carry `sessionFile`, so hostd reads subagent transcripts from disk. Bridge ≥ 2.2.0 adds `nav` to the requests of a multi-question ask: the ask dialog goes back a question when hostd answers `nav.back` (§3.5 "Back").

**Schema dump mode** (bridge ≥ 1.2.0). With `TF_OMP_SCHEMA_OUT=<file>` in its environment at load, the extension does nothing else: it imports `@oh-my-pi/pi-coding-agent/config/all-settings` (literal specifier; older omp builds without it report `error`), writes `{bridgeVersion, ompVersion, settings: <definition>[] | null, error?}` atomically to the file (functions dropped by JSON) and exits right after loading. Only tf-hostd's throwaway omp (§3.10) sets the variable.

**Settings-write mode** (bridge ≥ 1.3.0). With `TF_OMP_SETTINGS_IN=<file>` and `TF_OMP_SETTINGS_OUT=<file>` at load, the extension likewise does nothing else: it reads `{changes: [{key, text | null}]}` from the owner-only input file and deletes it at once, applies the changes in order through omp's Settings API (`config/settings` → `Settings.instance`, `config/all-settings` → `orderedSettings()`: `parse(text)` → `set`, or `unset`; then `flush`), exactly what `omp config set|reset` does, stops at the first failure, writes `{bridgeVersion, results: [{key, ok, error?}], error?}` atomically to the output file and exits. Values are never echoed. Only tf-hostd's throwaway omp sets the variables, for credential writes (§3.10).

## 5. App side

### 5.1 `tf-transport` (owner: AppTransport)

```rust
#[async_trait] pub trait Transport: Send + Sync {
    async fn exec(&self, cmd: &Cmd) -> Result<Output, TransportError>;       // one-shot (stdin optional, timeout)
    async fn spawn(&self, cmd: &Cmd) -> Result<Child, TransportError>;       // long-lived stdio (attach, uploads)
    async fn forward_tcp(&self, host: &str, port: u16) -> Result<BoxedStream, TransportError>; // previews
    fn kind(&self) -> HostKind;
    async fn close(&self);
}
pub struct Cmd { pub argv: Vec<String>, pub env: Vec<(String, String)>, pub cwd: Option<String>, pub stdin: Option<Vec<u8>>, pub timeout: Option<Duration> }
pub async fn connect(profile: &HostProfile, opts: &ConnectOptions, ev: mpsc::Sender<TransportEvent>) -> Result<Arc<dyn Transport>, TransportError>; // opts: known_hosts path (TOFU), timeouts
pub enum TransportEvent { Phase(ConnPhase, Option<String>), AuthUrl(String), Latency(u32), Closed(Option<String>) }
```

**SSH:**
- russh with the `ring` backend.
- Sign-in per `HostProfile.auth` (`ssh/auth.rs`):
  - `tailscale` (default, recommended): `none` auth. A banner containing a `login.tailscale.com` URL becomes `AuthUrl` and the `authRequired` phase; keep waiting up to 30 min. Other methods offered instead → `NotTailscaleSsh`.
  - `key` / `password` (Advanced: authentication in the host editor): the credential is loaded first (key from `keyPath` on this device or the key saved in the app, decrypted with the saved passphrase; OpenSSH, PEM PKCS#1/PKCS#8/SEC1, PuTTY), then `none` learns what the host offers, then the configured credential is tried once (a password also answers a `keyboard-interactive` password prompt). No agent, default key files or other secrets are ever tried. Failures name the method and cause and are not retryable: `passwordRejected`, `keyRejected` (fingerprint), `authMethodNotAllowed`, `keyPassphraseNeeded`, `keyPassphraseWrong`, `keyUnreadable`, `credentialMissing`, `authIncomplete`, `signInClosed`.
  - Host key checks (TOFU via `hosts.trustKey` outside the tailnet) run in the handshake, before any credential is sent, for every method.
- Secrets (`HostSecrets`: password, imported key, passphrase) are write-only: `hosts.save` / `hosts.test` take them beside the profile (`HostSaveParams.secrets`; absent = keep, empty = delete), the app core keeps them in `data_dir/secrets.json` (0600, keyed by host id; only what the method uses), `hosts.secretStatus` reports which exist, `hosts.remove` deletes them. They never appear in `hosts.json`, results, events, logs or the state cache.
- Our own keepalive runs after login (avoid the russh `keepalive_interval` bug).
- Commands run in the user's login shell (PATH includes `~/.local/bin`) without that shell ever parsing them (`quote.rs`). The exec request is `exec sh -c '<bootstrap>' sh <base64>`: the fixed bootstrap decodes the script (`base64 -d`, then `base64 -D`, `openssl`, `python3`, `perl`; the usual directories are appended to PATH only while decoding) and runs it in `sh`; the script exports the POSIX-quoted command as `TF_EXEC` and starts `$SHELL -lc 'exec sh -c '\''eval "$TF_EXEC"'\'''` (that one line reads the same in sh, bash, zsh, ksh, fish and nushell; any other `$SHELL`, e.g. csh, tcsh, elvish, xonsh or pwsh, is replaced by `sh -l`), and that `sh` runs the command. Every level `exec`s the next. Before 0.7.0 the POSIX-quoted command itself went to `$SHELL -lc`: nushell split the deploy script at `'\''`, and every deploy to such a host failed with `sha256sum: 4: Syntax error: Unterminated quoted string`.

**Local:** `tokio::process`, no shell.

**WSL:**
- Commands run as `wsl.exe -d <distro> --exec sh -c <bootstrap> sh <base64>`, the SSH words above as argv (`--exec`: no Linux-side `$SHELL -c` layer). Every argument is plain (`quote::is_plain`: printable ASCII and spaces, no quotes or backslashes), because wsl.exe gets one Windows command line: WSL from the Store splits it with `CommandLineToArgvW`, which reverses Rust's quoting, but other builds may not, and plain words split the same under every rule set (the unit tests simulate five). A distro name wsl.exe would read back differently fails with a `Config` error. The warm-up in `connect` runs `wsl.exe -d <distro> --exec true` itself, without the login shell, whose profile files can cost seconds on a slow laptop.
- `wsl.exe -l -v` and `wsl.exe -l --running -q` (run at once) list distros. Previews use plain localhost (WSL2 forwards it).
- **Keepalive.** WSL stops a distro `instanceIdleTimeout` (default 15 s) after its last `wsl.exe` client exits, and tf-hostd, herdr and every agent in it with it; `attach`, the app's only long-lived client, ends with every reconnect. `WslKeepalive` holds one client, `wsl.exe -d <distro> --exec sleep 2147483647` (no shell; 68 years instead of `sleep infinity`, which old busybox lacks, so nothing needs a fallback), and kills it on drop. tf-client keeps one per wanted WSL host (§5.2). Agents that must outlive the app (Disconnect, quit) need this in `%UserProfile%\.wslconfig`, applied after `wsl --shutdown`:
  ```ini
  [general]
  instanceIdleTimeout=-1
  ```

### 5.2 `tf-client` (owner: AppCore)
- **Engine API:**
  ```rust
  Engine::start(cfg: EngineConfig, platform: Arc<dyn Platform>) -> (Engine, broadcast::Receiver<AppEvent>)
  engine.call(host_id: Option<String>, method: &str, params: Value) -> Result<Value, ApiError>
  ```
  - Host methods require `host_id` and are forwarded to that host's hostd.
  - App methods are handled locally.
- **`Platform` trait:**
  - `notify(host, &Notification, style)` (one live OS notification per agent; a newer one replaces it)
  - `retract(host_id, &Notification)` (remove it unless a newer one of the agent replaced it)
  - `open_external(url)`
  - `set_keep_alive(bool)`
  - `device_name()`
  - `set_badge(BadgeState)`
- **Notification sync** ("handled in one place = done everywhere"). The engine remembers the last 100 notifications per host with their state (posted to the OS, read, resolved) and reports changes as `AppEvent::NotifyDone { hostId, ids, resolved }`:
  - **Needs-you, from host state.** After every snapshot/patch, a needs-you notification whose request left the state (the agent's `needs.requestId` went away or changed, or the agent is gone or archived) is resolved, whoever handled it: inline card, toast, board quick answer, Answer-next HUD, OS action or reply, another device, the omp TUI, cancel/stop. Resolution needs the request to have been seen pending first (hostd publishes the state before the notification). Its OS notification is retracted and the UI dismisses the toast and shows the list item as resolved.
  - **Read.** `app.notifyRead { hostId, agentId? }` (the UI calls it when an agent's session is viewed with the app in front, when one of the host's own notifications (no agent, such as "Closed 3 idle shells") is opened from its toast or the bell, with `agentId: ""`, which reads every host-level one of that host since no session view ever would, on "mark all read" and on "clear"), an OS body tap (`notification_action` without action) and an OS dismissal (`notification_dismissed`) mark notifications read; read ones are retracted from the OS, except needs-you ones whose request is still pending. The UI dismisses read toasts except pending needs-you ones.
  - **Fail safe.** OS answers (`notification_action` with an action or reply) to a needs-you notification whose request is resolved or no longer the pending one fail with `stale` instead of reaching the next request.
  - Removing a host retracts all its notifications. Cross-device sync is what host state carries: needs-you resolution; read state is per device. The ring lives in memory, so OS notifications left by a previous process (Android) are not retracted.
- **Per-host connection state machine.** Phases: connecting → authenticating → (authRequired) → deploying → starting → online. On failure: reconnecting with backoff 1, 2, 4 … 30 s plus jitter, reset after 60 s online. It retries immediately on `hosts.connect`, window focus, network changes and foreground. Only non-retryable errors stop it (`error` phase); a WSL start failure is retryable unless WSL or the distro is missing (a cold or busy VM fails `wsl.exe` now and then). A deploy step the host can never run stops it too (`deploy_failed`: its shell rejects the script, or a program the setup needs is missing or not executable; only exit 2, 3, 126 or 127 with the matching message in the last three lines of stderr, so profile noise at the top never turns a transient failure fatal; `deploy.rs` `deterministic`), as does a deployed tf-hostd that reports another version (`version_mismatch`), while timeouts, wsl.exe's own errors, checksum mismatches and a full disk keep retrying. The `error` card offers **Copy diagnostics** (app version and platform, host kind and address, phase, attempt, the error code, message and hint).
- **WSL keepalive.** From a WSL host's first connect attempt until it is no longer wanted (Disconnect, a permanent error, removal, quit), its host task holds one `WslKeepalive` for the distro (§5.1), restarted at the next attempt if it ended and moved when the distro changes; reconnects and backoff therefore never leave WSL without a client long enough to stop the distro.
- **Deploy:**
  1. Run `uname -sm` to pick the embedded hostd for that arch.
  2. Compare `~/.teleflock/bin/tf-hostd --version`. If the host's tf-hostd is **newer** than this build (another device runs a newer app), stop here: upload nothing, attach to it as is. A protocol this app doesn't speak fails with `version_mismatch` and an "update TeleFlock on this device" hint. Every remote step runs through a login shell (WSL: a `wsl.exe` start each); the probe may take 60 s.
  3. If it differs, upload through `exec` with stdin: a temp file whose sha256 must match, `chmod +x`, then its `--version` must print the expected line before `mv` replaces the installed binary (else `version_mismatch`, and the old binary stays). Local hosts are written directly and run `--version` afterwards (30 s; a run that cannot finish in time is retryable, not `version_mismatch`).
  4. Upload the extension and the overlay (with the relay port) when they differ, in one `exec` (the extension on stdin, the few lines of overlay inline in the script).
  5. Then `spawn tf-hostd attach --session S` (`--replace` after an upload, §3.2).

  A steady WSL connect is 3 `wsl.exe` starts (warm-up without a login shell, probe, attach); after an app update 5: those plus the hostd upload (which checks its own `--version`) and one for the extension and the overlay (7 before 0.5.1).
- **Protocol client.**
  - Hello (asking for the `zstd` and `sessionSync` features) and welcome; with `zstd` accepted, both directions switch to zstd streams right after the welcome (`link.rs`; an older hostd answers without features and everything stays plain). The link counts bytes on the wire and as JSON (`Link::totals`, logged when it closes).
  - Relays snapshot/patch, session, term, pulse and notify messages as `AppEvent`s.
  - Correlates requests (30 s default timeout; `term.open` 15 s; 180 s for methods that may launch an omp agent: `agent.start`, `agent.resume`, `agent.archive`, `agent.prompt`, `host.resume`, `worktree.create`, `automation.run`). The welcome may take 60 s (attach's 30 s daemon start plus the herdr session context).
  - Pings every 15 s (latency). **Liveness by unanswered pings:** the link closes once nothing at all arrived since a ping sent 30 s ago, i.e. at most 45 s after the host went silent. The tick pings before it judges anything, and a tick that comes a whole period late (system sleep, which Windows' monotonic clock counts; a stopped or starved app) judges nothing sent before it, so waking from sleep keeps a healthy link instead of reconnecting. The timestamps are tokio's monotonic clock, never the wall clock.
  - **Resume checks** (`Engine::resume(Resume)`): window focus (desktop, every alt-tab) pings only links the host has been quiet on for 20 s and gives them 15 s (a busy WSL VM is slow, not gone); Android resume and network changes ping every live link with 5 s. A link that misses its deadline reconnects.
- **Cache.** The last `HostState` per host is stored in `data_dir/cache/<hostId>.json` and emitted as `HostSnapshot` with `status.phase` ≠ online before the live connection is up (the UI marks it stale). Compact JSON, serialized from the live state under its lock; written only when something besides a health sample changed, at most every 60 s, and on every disconnect and at shutdown.
- **Session mirrors** (`sessions.rs`, with a hostd that speaks `sessionSync`, §3.3). Every session the UI opens goes through a per-host mirror: `session.open` sends what the mirror holds (`have`: the agent's omp session id from host state, its newest `limit` rows with hashes, the meta hash) and merges the delta; the mirror applies the live stream, verifying `seq` and append offsets (a gap, a wrong offset or a delta naming a row it lacks re-opens with `have`, silently; more than 3 re-opens a minute fall back to a plain subscription), and hands the UI the plain events it always got (`reset`, `upsert`, `append`, `remove`, `meta` — a `metaPatch` becomes a `meta` without the tree, which the UI keeps; a `treePatch` a `meta` with it). The mirror keeps the session tree apart from the meta and shared, so a `metaPatch` never copies or re-serializes it. Session messages that arrive while an open is in flight wait in the mirror until its snapshot says where they start. Opens are serialized per agent (the UI's, the re-open after a reconnect, a repair); an open of a session already live on the link is answered from the mirror (another devserver tab, the UI re-opening after the reconnect re-open). An open the UI or a page close abandons while it waits for hostd releases the subscription hostd made for it (`session.close`). A re-open that finds no answer or no session while the link stays up (the bridge reconnecting) is tried again after 1 s, twice as long each time up to 30 s, as long as the UI has the session open; one that cannot work, or a session that ended while it re-opened, reaches the UI as `ended` with the reason. Rows older than the synced window stay while the window's first row is held unchanged (the same entry has the same past), so `session.history` pages fetched once are served from the mirror (a partial page is completed from hostd); an oldest row hostd answers `not_found` for (a live-only notice of an earlier tf-hostd) is dropped and paging goes on from the next. Closed mirrors stay in memory up to a size budget per host (their JSON: 32 MiB, 8 MiB on Android), least recently used leaving first; a mirror an open, a re-open, a cache write or a history page is working on never leaves. With an older hostd, opens and events pass through and the mirror only remembers what to re-open.
- **Session cache** (`session_cache.rs`). Mirrors are written per host and omp session to `data_dir/sessions/<hostId>/<sessionId>.json.zst` (zstd JSON, at most 3000 rows; the directories 0700 and the files 0600 on Unix: tool output can hold secrets) when the UI closes them, at a disconnect, at a page close and at shutdown, and while open on a 15 s tick when they changed (rows, meta, tree; not a dialog, a side answer or a re-open that brought nothing new), a big one at most once per 15 s per MiB (up to 2 min); files beyond the budget (64 MiB on Android, 256 MiB elsewhere) are evicted least recently used first (reads count as use, file times carry it across restarts); `hosts.remove` deletes the host's dir. A file carries its format and the app version that wrote it: rows and meta keep hostd's hashes, and another app version may keep other fields of them, so another version's file is a miss. After an app restart, opening a cached session emits its cached rows as a `reset` at once (when host state says hostd has it live), then the delta lands as the open's result. **Offline opens** (`engine.rs` `session_open`, `sessions.rs` `open_offline`/`history_offline`): while the host's link is not up (connecting, deploying tf-hostd, offline), `session.open` is answered from the mirror or the cache at once, with every control off and no dialog (`offline_caps`; the UI shows the copy under LinkGate's "Showing the copy saved on this device" strip with the composer disabled), `session.history` from the rows the mirror holds, and `session.close` closes the mirror locally; once the link is up the mirror re-opens with `have` and the delta arrives as a `reset` with the real controls; a session nothing is held of waits for the link (up to 15 s while tf-hostd deploys or starts). A close (the UI's or a page close) that lands while an open waits (for the link, its turn or the cache) wins: the engine takes the open's ticket as the request arrives (`OpenTicket`: the mirror, held, and the closes it had seen), so the open answers `not_connected`, is not open and sends hostd nothing, and no later link subscribes it (a subscription hostd made while the open waited for its answer is released, here and at hostd). Re-opens after a reconnect and repairs carry the count they chose to open with; every open checks it under the lock that marks it in flight, on each of its passes (`sync_open`: a re-open after a gap or a failed merge is a pass of its own; the plain re-open of an older hostd checks it before it counts as live), and a cancelled open releases what hostd subscribed on any pass, so a close never leaves a subscription behind, nor a mirror the next open would take for live without one. In the UI only the latest open of a session sets its state (`client.ts` `loadSession`), so an earlier one failing late changes nothing. Lab (`sessions.rs` `lab_wire_bytes`, a real 1190-entry session): first open 428 KB → 78 KB, re-open from the cache 477 KB → 3 KB, reconnect mid-stream 475 KB → 3 KB.
- **Mirror self-heal** (`sessions.rs` `heal_due`, on the 15 s session tick; `engine.rs` `turn_ended`). An open mirror that shows a turn at work (a running tool, a streaming reply among its newest 64 rows, after the last row that ended a run: what a run left in flight before it ended never finishes) for 30 s while host state says the turn ended (the card in review or done, and hostd's own copy with no tool open and nothing streaming; a card that sits in review while omp works, under a herdr without omp's integration, does not count), and that heard nothing from hostd for 30 s, missed the messages that settled it, whatever lost them (a subscription hostd no longer had, as in the 0.6.1 open/close race). It is re-opened with what it holds (`session.open {have}`: hostd subscribes it again and the UI gets the delta as a `reset`), with a warning in the log. The next one waits 60 s, then 120 s, doubling up to 15 min while it keeps finding the same (15 min without one start the count over); a copy that hears from hostd is left alone. hostd's own feed watchdog (§3.4 "Stalled feed") repairs hostd's copy. Lab check (real omp, tf-devserver, every `session` message from hostd dropped from mid-turn on): the transcript stayed on the running `wait` until one re-open, 47 s after omp's TUI showed the final answer, brought it.
- **A UI that lagged** (`engine.rs` `resend_sessions`, `sessions.rs` `resend`; called by `src-tauri/src/core.rs` and `tf-devserver` on `RecvError::Lagged`). An event subscriber that fell 8192 events behind (the terminal of a redrawing TUI shares the stream) gets host state replayed, which covers the board but not the transcripts it lost events of: every mirror the UI has open on a `sessionSync` subscription is emitted again as a `reset` of its window, under the mirror's lock (in order with its other events); a plain subscription's events go straight to the UI, so what its mirror holds is older than the UI's and is not sent. Before (0.7.0), the board went on after such a lag while the open transcript stayed where the lag hit, and no repair upstream could see it: the mirror itself was complete.
- **Tunnels.** `tunnel.open` binds `127.0.0.1:0` (or exactly `localPort` for sign-ins, §3.14) and pipes each accepted connection over `transport.forward_tcp`.
- **Forwarding** (`forward.rs`, hostd side §3.30). Rules live in `data_dir/forwards.json` (never in `hosts.json`); `forward.save` validates them (the messages `src/core/forward.ts` shares with the editor and the demo), `hosts.remove` drops the rules that name the host. On every link up or down, rule change and host rename, each from-host with the `forward` feature gets the offers of its enabled rules (only when they changed); `fwdOffered` feeds the status. An `open` from a from-host is checked against the saved rule (enabled, shell / tool / port allowed, at most `FWD_STREAMS` open, its id not in use) and the to-host's link (connected, speaking `forward`), then opened on the to-host under a new odd id with the rule's target port, all under one lock, so a rule turned off, removed or stopped meanwhile either refuses the open or ends the stream after it; refusals go back as `close` with the reason. A to-host stream the app does not relay (any event but an ack or its end) gets a `close`. The relay holds no payload: each event goes to the other link as it arrives, counting bytes (`bytes_up` / `bytes_down`) and what each side has unacknowledged (past the window, or an ack of more than was passed: both ends are closed). A link that drops ends its streams (the caller learns "TeleFlock lost the connection to <to-host>"; the to-host gets a close and stops the program); a rule turned off, removed or changed so it no longer allows a stream ends that stream; `forward.stop` ends one. Status per rule: `disabled`, `waiting` (a host not connected), `unsupported` (a tf-hostd without `forward`), `error` (another app relays the name), else `ready` with port errors. `AppEvent::Forwards` after every change; byte counters at most every 2 s.
- **TeleFlock Browser** (`browser/`; host side §3.33, UI §6.13; wire `tf_proto::browser`). One Chromium-family browser per desktop app core (Windows, macOS, Linux, tf-devserver); an Android core answers `browser.status` with `unavailable` and every other `browser.*` with `unsupported`, and its hello does not ask for the `browser` feature (hosts then tell an asking agent at once that no browser is available).
  - **Engine** (`discover.rs`, `install.rs`). `browser.configSet {enginePath}` wins; else the first found of Google Chrome, Chromium, Microsoft Edge, Brave (Linux: PATH names, then `/opt/…`, `/usr/lib/…`; snap packages and Ubuntu's snap wrapper skipped, their confinement keeps them out of the hidden data dir; macOS: `/Applications` and `~/Applications`; Windows: `Program Files`, `Program Files (x86)`, `LOCALAPPDATA`), then a downloaded Chrome for Testing. Versions come from `--version` (Windows: the install's newest `x.y.z.w` directory), cached per path and modification time; a configured path is named by the product it reports. `browser.installEngine` downloads the pinned Chrome for Testing 156.0.8078.80 for the device's OS and CPU (linux64, linux-arm64, mac-arm64, mac-x64, win64) from `storage.googleapis.com/chrome-for-testing-public` (rustls with the webpki roots), refuses it unless size and SHA-256 match the pins, unpacks it to `<data_dir>/browser/engine/<version>/` and removes older versions; progress (0–95 downloading, then unpacking) and a failure are `BrowserStatus.install`. With no engine the status is `unavailable` with what to do.
  - **Process** (`run.rs`, `launch.rs`). One supervisor task owns it. Start: `<data_dir>/browser/profile.lock` (a file lock: one app core per profile; a second one answers "another TeleFlock on this device runs the browser with this profile"), staged files removed, the downloads folder created, a browser an earlier run left on the profile asked to quit (`Browser.close` on the port its `DevToolsActivePort` names) or, on Unix, ended by signal when its command line names the profile (Chromium would hand a new launch to it). Flags: `--user-data-dir=<profile>`, `--remote-debugging-port=0`, `--remote-debugging-address=127.0.0.1`, `--remote-allow-origins=` the app's origins (`tauri://localhost`, `http://tauri.localhost`, `https://tauri.localhost`) plus the embedder's (`Engine::allow_browser_origins`: tf-devserver's served origin and every `--allow-origin`; a debug Tauri build's `devUrl`), `--no-first-run`, `--no-default-browser-check`, `--disable-search-engine-choice-screen`, `--hide-crash-restore-bubble`, no background throttling (agents' tabs are never in front), the same secrets store in both modes (Linux `--password-store=basic`, macOS `--use-mock-keychain`, what headless picks by itself, so cookies stay readable across modes), `--no-sandbox` only when the app runs as root, and `--headless=new --window-size=1280,800` unless headed. The DevTools port and the browser target's path are read from `DevToolsActivePort` (30 s); `BrowserStatus.wsUrl` is `ws://127.0.0.1:<port>/devtools/browser/<id>` (the webview cannot read `/json/version`). Its own process group (Linux: SIGTERM when the app core's thread that started it dies). `browser.start {headed}` relaunches it in the other mode (same profile); a headed browser whose window the user closed (exit 0) comes back headless. A crash or any other exit restarts it after 1, 2, 4, 8, 16 s (a run of 60 s starts the count again); the sixth consecutive one leaves it in `error` until a start. Stop (`browser.stop`, `clearData everything`, `Engine::shutdown`): `Browser.close` (a clean exit, nothing to restore next time), SIGTERM after 5 s, a kill after 3 more, then the rest of its process group; `browser.stop` first detaches every agent (their hosts are told) and puts waiting requests back to pending.
  - **Attachments** (`agents.rs`, `streams.rs`, `mod.rs`). `browser.attach {hostId, agentId, overCap?}` (scope `session`) and an allowed request (scope as answered, `turn` for Allow once) claim a slot (at most `cap` agents attached or being attached, `browser.configSet {cap}` 1–16, default 4; else `browser_busy` "4 agents use the browser"), start the browser when needed, register the agent with the proxy, then call the host's `agent.browserAttach {agentId, port, scope}` (`port`: the one it had, so the agent's `browser.cdpUrl` stays the same). Each connection the agent opens on that port arrives as `fwd open {kind: browser, name: <agentId>}` (even sid); one from an agent not attached from this app (or being attached: hostd may hand it over before this app read its answer) is closed with the reason. Accepted ones answer `opened` and run through an in-memory pipe into `CdpProxy::serve` (`browser/proxy/`): bytes towards the agent within the credit hostd acknowledges (`FWD_WINDOW`, frames of at most `FWD_CHUNK`), the agent's bytes acknowledged once the proxy took them; a peer past the window, an over-acknowledgement or bad base64 cuts the stream. It ends when both directions ended, on `close`, with its link or when the proxy closes it. `browser.detach`, `browserDetached` from the host (turn ended, omp session ended, another device took it) and a lost link end attachments here (the proxy closes the agent's connections; its tabs stay as detached); a lost link ends only the turn-scoped ones made on it: session-scoped ones attach again on the next link with the same port, turn-scoped ones the old link's end missed end there. `BrowserAttachment.connections` counts the open streams, `activeAt` and `tabs` come from the proxy (names filled in here, kept for detached agents).
  - **DevTools proxy** (`proxy/`: `registry.rs` owners, `monitor.rs`, `filter.rs`, `http.rs`, `session.rs`). Every connection an attached agent opens is served by `CdpProxy::serve`, never by the browser's port; an agent that is not attached gets 403. HTTP as Chrome answers it: `/json/version` (its `webSocketDebuggerUrl` is `ws://<the request's Host>/devtools/browser/<id>`, so Puppeteer's `browserURL` works through hostd's port), `/json/list` (= `/json`) with only the agent's pages, `PUT /json/new?<url>` (a tab of its own), `/json/close/<id>` and `/json/activate/<id>` on its pages only (else 404 `No such target id`), `/json/protocol`; a `Host` that is neither an IP address nor `localhost` (DNS rebinding) and a WebSocket upgrade carrying an `Origin` are refused, as Chrome does. A WebSocket upgrade to `/devtools/browser/…` gets one WebSocket of its own to the browser target (`/devtools/page/<id>`: to one of its pages), and every JSON-RPC message both ways passes the connection's filter:
    - **Owners** (`registry.rs`). Ownership is per page (the target id `BrowserTabOwner.targetId` names): a page is an agent's when it opened it (`Target.createTarget`, `/json/new`; always `newWindow: true`, so agents' tabs paint headless and never fight over the user's active tab), when one of its pages opened it (`openerId`: popups, `window.open`, links with `target=_blank`), or when the user lent it (`browser.lendTab`); everything else is the user's. Chrome's `tab` targets (the parents of pages) belong to the owner of the page they show; a page a tab holds without showing it (`subtype`: prerendered by speculation rules) is that tab owner's, and once activated it is the tab's page and keeps the owner (a cancelled one takes nothing with it). The proxy's own connection (`monitor.rs`) discovers every target and auto-attaches tabs, then each tab's page, without `waitForDebuggerOnStart`, detaching the page at once: it learns which page each tab shows and pauses nothing. When it connects again to the same browser it reports every target afresh; owners of pages that closed meanwhile go, and nobody's sessions are judged until it has reported them all.
    - **Visibility.** `Target.getTargets`, `targetCreated`/`targetInfoChanged`/`targetDestroyed`, `attachedToTarget`/`detachedFromTarget` list only the agent's tabs and pages, the frames and dedicated workers it attached under them, and its connection's own browser target (attachable, as Puppeteer's `browser.target().createCDPSession()` needs; not closable). Service and shared workers are not its. `attachToTarget`, `activateTarget`, `closeTarget`, `getTargetInfo`, `Browser.getWindowForTarget` naming any other target answer Chrome's `No target with given id found` (-32602), so the user's and other agents' tabs simply do not exist for it; messages tagged with a session it does not hold are dropped, commands on one answer `Session with given id not found.` (-32001). Browser contexts and windows: only the ones it created or that hold its tabs.
    - **Auto-attach.** A target Chrome attaches for the agent's auto-attach that is not its own is released by the proxy at once (`Runtime.runIfWaitingForDebugger` when it waits, then `Target.detachFromTarget`), so `waitForDebuggerOnStart` never pauses the user's or another agent's new tab. A target that cannot be decided yet (a tab whose page the monitor has not reported, a page that may be the answer of a `Target.createTarget` on its way, a popup of the agent's page not adopted yet) is held back, still paused, with its events in order, until it is decided, at most 3 s: then it is released (and dropped from discovery); should it turn out to be the agent's within 30 s, the proxy attaches it for the agent (or announces it) then.
    - **Refused** (`{code: -32000, message: "TeleFlock refused `<method>`: <why>"}`): `Browser.*` except versions, the command line, histograms and windows of its own tabs (`Browser.close`, `crash`, `crashGpuProcess`, …), `Browser.setDownloadBehavior` and permissions outside a context the agent created, `Page.setDownloadBehavior`, `Target.disposeBrowserContext` of a context it did not create, `Target.exposeDevToolsProtocol`, non-flat sessions (`sendMessageToTarget`, `flatten` false), `Storage.clearCookies`/`clearDataForOrigin`/`clearDataForStorageKey` and the other wipes, `Network.clearBrowserCookies`/`clearBrowserCache`/`setCookieControls`, `CacheStorage.deleteCache`, `IndexedDB.deleteDatabase`, `DOMStorage.clear`, `ServiceWorker.*` (but enable/disable), `Tethering`, `Tracing`, `Extensions`, `PWA`, `Cast`; files of the device (`DOM.setFileInputFiles`, `Network.loadNetworkResource`, `Input.dispatchDragEvent` with files); `Target.createTarget`/`Page.navigate` to anything but web pages (`http`, `https`, `data`, `javascript`, `blob:` of a web origin, `about:blank`: no `chrome:`, `file:`, `devtools:`, extensions, other browsers' schemes); and on the browser target any per-page domain (`Fetch`, `Network`, `Security`, `Emulation`, …: agents send those to their own tabs). Everything else on its own tabs passes untouched.
    - **The user in charge.** `browser.takeOver` freezes the agent's sessions on that tab: each command there answers `the user took over this tab` (-32000), their events stay with the proxy, the tab leaves its lists, and a page connection to it closes with that reason. The tab's own session (the one Puppeteer auto-attaches tabs with) stays attached in the browser, since Puppeteer closes its page object for good when that session ends; the page sessions under it, and any other session on the tab, are detached in the browser, and whatever the browser auto-attaches under the kept tab session meanwhile (a page the user's browsing prerenders) is run and detached unseen. `browser.handBack` attaches the tab's current page again under the kept tab session, then tells the agent its old sessions under the tab ended (`detachedFromTarget`, innermost first, each on its parent) and delivers the new page session the way Chrome activates a prerendered page, the one way Puppeteer moves a live page to another session: `attachedToTarget` under the tab session with `subtype: "prerender"`, then `targetInfoChanged` of the page without the subtype (a `targetCreated` first when the tab's page changed meanwhile; a client without discovery gets a plain attach). The agent's page object, frames and handles carry on. Without a kept tab session (pages attached directly), or when the browser refuses the attach, the sessions end and the tab is attached again where the agent's auto-attach would have. A tab closed while the user has it ends the agent's sessions there (it hears them end). `browser.lendTab` makes a user's tab the agent's (its auto-attach attaches it, its discovery announces it); taking a lent tab over gives it back to the user for good: the agent's sessions there end, in the browser too, and lending it again attaches it again on the same connection; a detach gives back every tab lent to that agent. `CdpProxy::detach` closes the agent's connections; the tabs it opened stay open, `detached`, until closed (an agent attached again gets them back). `set_upstream` (a restarted browser) and `close_all` (stopped) end every agent connection and forget the targets; attachments stay.
    - **First tab and activity.** An agent's first browser connection while it owns no live tab opens a blank one for it: omp's `browser.cdpUrl` mode drives a tab the browser already has and gives up after 10 s when it sees none. Commands mark the agent and its tab active (`BrowserAttachment.activeAt`, `BrowserTabOwner.activeAt`, at most every 2 s); `CdpProxy::changes` ticks on every owner change and on activity.
  - **Requests.** Pending `use_user_browser` requests are read from every live board (`Agent.browser.request`; a cached state has none) after each snapshot and each patch that is not only health. `browser.answer {requestId, allow: false}` → the host's `agent.browserAnswer {allow: false}`; `allow` attaches with `scope` (default `session`), which answers the request on the host; at the cap the request waits (`BrowserRequest.waiting` and its scope) and attaches oldest first when a slot frees (a detach, a lost link, a raised cap), or at once with `overCap`; a request answered elsewhere or withdrawn leaves the queue. `agent.answer` of a browser needs-you question (`NeedsYou.browser`: choice `session` / `turn` / `deny`, or a dismissal) is routed to `browser.answer` by a core with the browser (so OS notification actions and the board's quick answer work); a core without it forwards it to the host, which takes only a deny.
  - **Files** (`files.rs`). Downloads go to the user's downloads folder (`~/Downloads` when the system names none; `BrowserStatus.downloadDir`); `browser.revealDownload {path}` shows a file under it in the file manager (`open -R`, `explorer /select,`, `xdg-open` of its folder) and refuses any other path; `browser.revealProfile {}` shows the profile folder the same way (created first when the browser never ran). `browser.stageFile {name, data}` writes a picked file (≤ 32 MiB, base64) to `<data_dir>/browser/staged/<random>/<name>` (name reduced to its last component, no reserved characters) for `DOM.setFileInputFiles`; staged files go at every start and stop. `browser.clearData`: `cookies` (`Storage.clearCookies`) and `cache` (`Network.clearBrowserCache` on a page) over DevTools while it runs, else their files in the profile; `everything` stops it, deletes the profile and starts it again in the same mode.
  - **Config and events.** `<data_dir>/browser/config.json` (`enginePath`, `headed`: the mode the app starts it in, `cap`); `browser.config` probes the engines again. `AppEvent::Browser {status}` goes out when the status changed (process, engine, install progress, attachments, requests, connections; the proxy's tab owners and activity at most every 250 ms) and is part of every replay.
  - **Tests.** `cargo test -p tf-client browser`: discovery order per OS, flags, `DevToolsActivePort`, the restart schedule, the cap, queue and scopes over links, flow control of streams, staging and downloads paths, the profile lock, and with a real Chromium (`TF_TEST_CHROME`, else one installed, else Playwright's or Puppeteer's cache; skipped without one; `-- --skip live:: --skip real_browser_lifecycle --skip one_core_per_profile` leaves out the three tests that start one) start, the origins DevTools accept, a cookie through a restart, a crash restarted, nothing left after shutdown, one core per profile. `browser::proxy`: every rule above on DevTools traffic recorded from Chrome for Testing 156 with Puppeteer's auto-attach (lists, discovery, releasing the user's paused tab, holding an agent's new tab until its page is known and attaching it late when that took too long, a user's tab opened during an agent's `createTarget`, popups (of popups too, none for a page the user took over), prerendered pages, browser-target sessions, refusals, windows of an agent's own tabs only, contexts, take over and the hand back swap (with and without discovery, a page replaced meanwhile, a refused attach, again and again), targets let go under a taken-over tab, a tab closed while taken over or by the user, a lent tab taken back and lent again, lend (before the agent connects, listed, attached and driven), the same agent id on another host being another attachment, dialogs and file choosers of an agent's tab reaching it, an agent's activity noted at most every 2 s, page connections, the monitor's tab map and its reconnecting, HTTP heads, hosts and lists), served against a stand-in browser (`proxy/served.rs`: loopback `/json/*` and DevTools WebSockets that answer like Chrome's and record what they were asked; no browser runs): `/json/version` through the address the agent used and never the browser's, `/json/list` of its own pages only, `/json/new` (PUT only, web pages only, a window of its own, the agent's alone), `/json/close` and `/json/activate` on its own tabs only (a taken-over one answers with the reason), page connections (not for another's page, not for a web page's `Origin`, closed with the take-over reason), an agent's first tab (one per agent, not per connection), a detach ending its WebSockets and idle HTTP connections with its tabs kept, detached, and the agent refused until it attaches again, and against a real Chromium two agents through their own proxied connections: each opens and drives its tabs, sees only those (`Target.getTargets`, `/json/list`), cannot attach, activate or close the other's or the user's tab, `Browser.close` and `Storage.clearCookies` are refused, a tab the user opens while both auto-attach with `waitForDebuggerOnStart` loads, a popup is its opener's, take over fails the agent's next command on the page and on its tab session, hand back delivers the page on a new session under the same tab session as an activated prerender and it evaluates there, a lent tab is driven by its borrower only, a detach closes the connection and keeps the tabs. Lab: `scripts/lab/browser/` (`setup.sh --chrome <Chrome for Testing> --ui`, then `drive.ts`; 57 checks): tf-devserver with a real Chromium (else the pinned Chrome for Testing downloaded into the lab), a lab tf-hostd and real omp 18.15.0 agents (pinned into the lab, mock model) that drive the browser with omp's own `browser` (eval `browser.open`, `tab.text`, `tab.run` with Puppeteer) through tf-hostd's port and the proxy: (a) attach on purpose (the card follows once hostd published the board), the agent opens a page that sets a cookie and reads it, its DevTools and `/json/list` never show the user's own tab; (c) that cookie survives `browser.stop`/`start` (read again by the agent, sent to the site), detach keeps its tab detached and closes its port; (b) `use_user_browser`: the request in `browser.status`, the needs-you card and the notification, Allow for this session (the agent reads the cookie the other agent set), Deny (the declined text, no browser call), Allow once (omp's `browser.cdpUrl` is a session override during the turn, the attachment, card and port end with the turn, next turn the setting is back, the model read the bridge's note and its old tab fails at once); (d) two agents driving at once see only their own pages, `Browser.close` refused, a popup is its opener's and invisible to the other, take over fails the agent's next call with "the user took over this tab", hand back lets the same omp tab drive the page again, a detach keeps its tabs detached while the other agent goes on; the cap and its queue; SIGTERM to the devserver leaving no browser process. With `--ui` it screenshots the request card, the board and the tab strip grouped by owner (live dots while both drive, after a detach) at 1440×900 into the lab's `shots/`.
- **Dictation** (`dictation/`, feature `voice`; engine, models and UI §6.16; wire `tf_proto::dictation`). The desktop app (tf-client with `voice` on Windows, macOS and Linux) and devservers built with `--features voice` serve `dictation.*` on tf-dictation; every other core answers each method `unsupported` (the Android app, which keeps its system speech recognizer, and builds without the feature), and cores before 0.9.0 `unknown_method`. Calls run on the blocking pool (opening the microphone and listing devices block). `AppEvent::Dictation {state}` goes out on every change (download progress at most every 250 ms) and is part of every replay; a session's events go out as `AppEvent::DictationSession {session, event}` under the id the page chose. `Engine::page_closed` (a reload) cancels every session; `shutdown` cancels sessions and downloads (a download resumes later from its `.part` files).
- **Embedded artifacts.** `build.rs` embeds the binaries found under `$TF_HOSTD_DIST` (default `target/hostd-dist/<triple>/tf-hostd[.exe]`) plus `host/teleflock-bridge/teleflock-bridge.ts`. The devserver may override this with `--hostd-bin`.

### 5.3 `tf-devserver` (owner: AppCore)
`tf-devserver --bind 127.0.0.1:7420 --data-dir <dir> [--static dist] [--hostd-bin <path>] [--token <t>] [--allow-host <name[:port]>]… [--allow-origin <origin>]… [--allow-remote]`.

**Access control.** `/ws` drives every host, so it is gated against other web origins (CSWSH), DNS rebinding and other machines:
- **Token.** A random 256-bit token is generated per launch (`--token` overrides it; at least 22 chars of `[A-Za-z0-9_-]`). Startup prints `http://<bind>/?token=<t>`. `/ws` requires it as `?token=<t>` or as the subprotocol `teleflock.<t>` (echoed back); otherwise it answers 401.
- **Origin.** If present, `Origin` must be the served origin (`http[s]://<Host>`) or an `--allow-origin` (e.g. `http://localhost:5173` for a `vite dev` UI using `?core=`); otherwise 403. Non-browser clients without `Origin` need only the token.
- **Host.** Every route rejects (421) a `Host` other than the bind IP, `localhost` or a loopback IP on the bound port (any IP literal when bound to `0.0.0.0`/`::`), or an `--allow-host` name (`name` matches any port).
- **Remote.** A non-loopback `--bind` is refused unless `--allow-remote` is passed.
- **Index.** The served `index.html` (also for `/` and SPA routes, `Cache-Control: no-store`) gets `<meta name="tf-token" content="<t>">` after `<head>` when a loopback client addresses a loopback name, or when the page URL has the right `?token=`.

**WebSocket `/ws`:**
- **Client → server:** `{id, hostId?, method, params}`.
- **Server → client:**
  - `{id, result}` or `{id, error}`
  - `{event: AppEvent}`
  - a terminal input fast path: `{termIn: {hostId, termId, data}}`

**Static.** It serves the built UI, so a browser on the tailnet (with `--allow-remote`, `--allow-host` and the token URL) can run the full app against real hosts. This is used for verification.

**TeleFlock Browser.** The devserver runs the app core's browser (§5.2) on its own machine: the served origin (for a loopback bind also `http://localhost:<port>`, `http://127.0.0.1:<port>`, `http://[::1]:<port>`) and every `--allow-origin` may open the browser's DevTools, so the page (or a `vite dev` UI) shows it. SIGTERM shuts it down like Ctrl+C, which stops the browser with it.

**Dictation.** Built with `--features voice` (labs), the devserver runs the app core's dictation on its own machine (§6.16) and remembers the sessions each tab starts (`dictation.start`): they are cancelled when the tab goes. Debug builds play WAV files as the microphone (`TF_DICTATION_FAKE_MIC`: one file per capture in turn, joined like `PATH`) and can download models from a local mirror (`TF_DICTATION_MIRROR`), so the dictation lab (`scripts/lab/dictation/`) needs no sound card, and with `--models` no network either.

### 5.4 `src-tauri` (owner: TauriShell)
- **Core bridge.** Commands are `core_call(hostId?, method, params)` and `core_term_input(hostId, termId, data)`. `core_events(channel: Channel<AppEvent[]>)` subscribes the webview: the replay (with held UI intents), then the live events in order, in batches. Every channel send is a script evaluation in the webview (plus a fetch from 8 KB up), so each live event goes out with whatever queued behind it meanwhile; nothing waits for company (1,000 events in bursts of 50: 37 sends instead of 1,000). `src/core/transport/tauri.ts` hands each batch's events to its listeners one by one.
- **Drafts at the end of the process** (`drafts.rs`; app `src/core/{draftFlush,mirrored,drafts,draftSlots,draftVideos}.ts`). Pages keep unsent drafts in browser storage, but no page code runs when the shell ends the process itself: an update's restart, the Windows installer (exits the process), Quit from the tray (the AppImage installs a waiting update then), the Android installer (the system kills the app). Before each, the shell emits `tf-drafts://flush {id}` to every page that registered (`drafts_listen`; a page that starts loading is dropped) and waits for each `drafts_flushed {id}`, at most 3 s (`update::apply::install_and_restart`: before the Windows installer, after a Linux install right before `request_restart`; `drafts::quit`; Android `launch_apk`). A page answers once `flushAll` resolved: every draft store written and its IndexedDB transactions complete, after waiting up to 1.5 s for draft work in flight (an image being read, a paste waiting for its stored list: `trackDraftWork`).
